Assertiveness in Nursing: Enhancing Communication and Professional Roles

ASSERTIVENESS RUTH CHEMBA

https://pt.slideshare.net/slideshow/assertiveness-in-nursing-enhancing-communication-and-professional-roles/287084181#1

GENERAL OBJECTIVE • At

https://pt.slideshare.net/slideshow/assertiveness-in-nursing-enhancing-communication-and-professional-roles/287084181#2

the end of this lecture, student should gain knowledge on assertiveness and be able to practice assertive behaviour.

SPECIFIC OBJECTIVE • At

https://pt.slideshare.net/slideshow/assertiveness-in-nursing-enhancing-communication-and-professional-roles/287084181#3

the end of the lecture, students should be able to; 1. Define assertiveness 2. Explain assertiveness versus other styles of interpersonal behaviors 3. Describe the methods of learning assertive behavior 4. Describe techniques for practicing assertiveness 5. Explain barriers to assertive behavior

INTRODUCTION • Nursing is

https://pt.slideshare.net/slideshow/assertiveness-in-nursing-enhancing-communication-and-professional-roles/287084181#4

a profession that demands a lot of communication • Communicating with members of the health care team, with patients and their kin etc • A nurse therefore needs t learn how to interact in a way that is not offensive yet not too passive. • Thus the need for a nurse to cultivate assertive behaviour

DEFINITION • Assertiveness is

https://pt.slideshare.net/slideshow/assertiveness-in-nursing-enhancing-communication-and-professional-roles/287084181#5

the ability to express one's feelings and assert one's rights while respecting the feelings and rights of others (Bower, 1994).

ASSERTIVENESS VERSUS OTHER INTERPERSONAL

https://pt.slideshare.net/slideshow/assertiveness-in-nursing-enhancing-communication-and-professional-roles/287084181#6

BEHAVIOURS 1. Aggressiveness • Aggressiveness is expressing one's thoughts in a way that is inappropriate and violets the rights of others. • It involves putting our wants, needs and rights above those of others.

https://pt.slideshare.net/slideshow/assertiveness-in-nursing-enhancing-communication-and-professional-roles/287084181#7

an aggressive behavior? • The behavior that aims at getting your own way, even if that is at the expense of someone else. • Aggressive behavior offends or violates someone else's rights. • It is a showing, perhaps both physically and verbally of anger. • Can sometimes be an expression of fear, lack of self- esteem, or inability to control a situation in any other way.

- Places the

https://pt.slideshare.net/slideshow/assertiveness-in-nursing-enhancing-communication-and-professional-roles/287084181#8

rights of the aggressor above the rights of the other party and avoids responsibility. • Involves pushing people into doing things, attacking, blaming, and putting others down. Not listening, giving orders. • Deciding for others and they know it. • Overreacting. Reacting to threatening situations by attacking outrightly. • High tone of voice, shouting and screaming, Glaring eyes.

2. Submissiveness (Passiveness) •

https://pt.slideshare.net/slideshow/assertiveness-in-nursing-enhancing-communication-and-professional-roles/287084181#9

It is a tendency to obey without arguing or resisting, not participating actively and usually letting others make decisions. • What is a submissive behaviour? • Failing to stand up for your own rights or doing so in such a way that other people disregard them. • Often shown in: • Not wanting to say “no” to unreasonable requests, • Not wanting to draw attention to yourself, • A belief that you are not as good as others, • wanting to appear polite.

- Going along

https://pt.slideshare.net/slideshow/assertiveness-in-nursing-enhancing-communication-and-professional-roles/287084181#10

with others to keep the peace or in order to be liked. • Agreeing to do things you don't want to do without negotiating. • Complaining behind the scenes. • Letting others make decisions. • Putting yourself down, being over-apologetic. • Running away from confrontation situations. • Can't maintain eye contact, have folded arms behind.

3. Assertiveness • In

https://pt.slideshare.net/slideshow/assertiveness-in-nursing-enhancing-communication-and-professional-roles/287084181#11

assertiveness, it is clear that there are two players. • A person states what they want clearly, gently but firmly acknowledging own rights and those of others. • Standing up for oneself and those dependent on them. • Making own decisions and allowing others to make theirs. • Showing respect for yourself and others.

- Listening to

https://pt.slideshare.net/slideshow/assertiveness-in-nursing-enhancing-communication-and-professional-roles/287084181#12

others' view point without necessarily going along with it. • Giving praise and constructive criticism. • Taking responsibility for own feelings and decisions. • Helping others to be assertive, treating adults as adults. • One is able to maintain eye contact. Arms on the side.

LEARNING ASSERTIVE BEHAVIOUR •

https://pt.slideshare.net/slideshow/assertiveness-in-nursing-enhancing-communication-and-professional-roles/287084181#13

Decide that you want to be assertive and not aggressive or passive. Commitment is the biggest step • Include positive and negative information in a statement. i.e. “I like your plan but………………” • Start the statement with “I” and avoid generalizations. i.e. we believe………………………… • Express your own beliefs and rights i.e. “I believe that ………………” • Express your thoughts and feelings directly to reinforce your identity.

- Make assertive

https://pt.slideshare.net/slideshow/assertiveness-in-nursing-enhancing-communication-and-professional-roles/287084181#14

statements: A. Simple assertive, “I think…………….” B. Emphatic assertive, “I realize you are tired but………..” C. Confrontive assertive,” you said you would bath Mr C but you didn't………………” D. Soft assertive, “I agree with what most of you said but I also think………”

TECHNIQUES FOR PRACTICING ASSERTIVENESS •

https://pt.slideshare.net/slideshow/assertiveness-in-nursing-enhancing-communication-and-professional-roles/287084181#15

Techniques of assertiveness can vary widely. Manuel Smith, in his 1975 book (When I Say No, I Feel Guilty) offered some of the following behaviours: 1. Broken record • Consists of simply repeating your requests or your refusals every time you are met with resistance. • As with a broken record, the key to this approach is repetition

2. Fogging • Consists

https://pt.slideshare.net/slideshow/assertiveness-in-nursing-enhancing-communication-and-professional-roles/287084181#16

of finding some limited truth to agree with in what an antagonist is saying, more specifically, one can agree in part or agree in principle. 3. Negative assertion • Negative assertion is agreement with criticism, without letting up demand. 4. Distance and personal space • Don't let someone you don't know very much invade your space, it makes you uncomfortable.

5. Good time

https://pt.slideshare.net/slideshow/assertiveness-in-nursing-enhancing-communication-and-professional-roles/287084181#17

management • Being early for meetings and appointments. 6. Maintain eye contact • makes the listener feel respected 8. Sit up • Don't cross your legs or fold your arms. It's a sign of nervousness 9. Active listening • Repeat what the other person has said, keep it short and don't interrupt the speaker. 10. Tone of voice • An assertive person does not change the tone of voice when annoyed.

BARRIERS TO ASSERTIVE

https://pt.slideshare.net/slideshow/assertiveness-in-nursing-enhancing-communication-and-professional-roles/287084181#18

BEHAVIOUR 1. Anxiety 2. Modesty- Some fear that positive self statements seem ego centric 3. Good friend 4. Obligation 5. Gender role 6. Strength of an issue 7. Lack of self-esteem, meaning, “how I feel about myself 8. Inexperience 9. Lack of confidence

THE ROLE OF

https://pt.slideshare.net/slideshow/assertiveness-in-nursing-enhancing-communication-and-professional-roles/287084181#19

THE NURSE IN THE HEALTH CARE SYSTEM The role concept • The concept of the role refers to the general expectations held about appropriate behaviours. • Some expectations regarding the nursing role have remained valid over long periods of time but other newer nursing roles may as yet be largely unorganised by the public.

- With decentralisation

https://pt.slideshare.net/slideshow/assertiveness-in-nursing-enhancing-communication-and-professional-roles/287084181#20

of health services at all levels, the roles of a nurse have expanded to include preventive, curative, rehabilitative, and palliative. • This has empowered the nursing professionals to co-ordinate total care of needs of not only their clients/patients in hospital who are bed ridden but also has empowered nursing roles out side hospital • Some roles can be carried out at each and every level of service provision while others are best performed in specialized care settings. • Some of the basic roles include:-

1. Care Giver •

https://pt.slideshare.net/slideshow/assertiveness-in-nursing-enhancing-communication-and-professional-roles/287084181#21

The most basic role of the nurse. • The nurse provides both physical (bathing and feeding ) and psychological (counseling) care to the client to enhance the healing process and assist the client to regain independence 2. Client advocate • The nurse protects the client's human and legal rights and helping clients and families to interpret information from other health care providers

3. Care coordinator

https://pt.slideshare.net/slideshow/assertiveness-in-nursing-enhancing-communication-and-professional-roles/287084181#22

/manager • The Nurse coordinates the activities of other health care providers eg physiotherapist, manage the affairs of a ward, health centre or department. 4. Change agent • Nurses should lead by example in the communities they live e.g. health promotion activities such as cervical cancer screening, access to family planning, going for VCT. • Champion the cause for new and progressive strategies

5. Educator • As

https://pt.slideshare.net/slideshow/assertiveness-in-nursing-enhancing-communication-and-professional-roles/287084181#23

an educator, the nurse explains to the client concepts and facts about health, demonstrates procedures, reinforces learning or client's behavior and evaluate the learning process. • The nurse can also be an educator to other nurses and students. 6. Researcher • The Nurse Researcher investigates problem in order to improve nursing care. • A Nurse Researcher can be employed in an academic setting, hospital or non-governmental organization.

7. Health promoter/Protector:

https://pt.slideshare.net/slideshow/assertiveness-in-nursing-enhancing-communication-and-professional-roles/287084181#24

The nurse ensures that the activities that are directed toward developing a person's resources to maintain or enhance well being as a protection against illness are done. 8. Care planner: This process involves an assessment of the client's health needs across the health care continuum and then plan on how the nursing care is carried out..

9. Counsellor: The

https://pt.slideshare.net/slideshow/assertiveness-in-nursing-enhancing-communication-and-professional-roles/287084181#25

nurse is trained as a counsellor in various aspects of the nursing profession. She/he is capable of counselling the client on various topics such as HIV/AIDS counselling, Family Planning counselling, Genetic Counselling etc. 10. Decision Maker: The nurse makes decision on the best care that can be given to the patient under her/his care. For example she can make a decision on the number of nurses that are needed per shift or the resources that are needed in the ward or hospital.

Expanded roles of

https://pt.slideshare.net/slideshow/assertiveness-in-nursing-enhancing-communication-and-professional-roles/287084181#26

nurses by GNC • The provision of relevant therapeutic intervention after assessment and making a diagnosis. • Inserting and removing devices • Carrying out vacuum extraction • Carry out intravenous infusion procedures • Prescription of relevant drugs and other therapeutic preparations • Run Nursing Homes

REFERENCE • Bray, D.

https://pt.slideshare.net/slideshow/assertiveness-in-nursing-enhancing-communication-and-professional-roles/287084181#27

(2008). Knowledge Ecosystems: Technology, Motivations, Processes, and Performance, Emory University. • Bernard, P. (1992). Counselling handbook, London, J. Ranken. • Rowan, W, (1983).The effective communication skills. London, Lightbridge

Tactical Empathy for Modern Negotiating Success

December 3, 2020

Dan Pilat, Dr. Sekoul Krastev

Law · Psychology

Reading time

Chris Voss is a famous negotiator, professor, and businessman well-known for his high-profile FBI career, and his book

Never Split the Difference: Negotiating as if your life depended on it

. Voss has revolutionized negotiation tactics and greatly impacted how organizations and individuals globally understand and negotiate with one another.

Voss has developed negotiation tactics throughout his lengthy professional career, gaining respect from individuals across disciplines. Voss's primary philosophy in his negotiation processes surrounds the premise that individuals want to be understood and accepted, and that collaboration and empathy are at the core of good negotiations.

Persuasion is not about how bright or smooth or forceful you are. It's about the other party convincing themselves that the solution you want is their own idea. So don't beat them with logic or brute force. Ask them questions that open paths to your goals. It's not about you.

– Chris Voss, Never Split the Difference: Negotiating As If Your Life Depended On It

Tactical Empathy - The key to negotiation is engaging emotional intelligence

Tactical empathy is an innovative approach to negotiating. This strategy utilizes different aspects of behavioral science to the advantage of the negotiator. Tactical empathy was coined by Chris Voss, which he developed through his experiences as a professional negotiator for the Federal Bureau of Investigation. Tactical empathy is an approach centered around collaboration and empathy rather than aggressive and abrasive negotiation tactics.1 Voss refers to empathy as becoming utterly aware of the other person's perspective and understanding their viewpoints and emotions. Simply put, tactical empathy is the act of understanding another persons' mindset and feelings and making them feel understood.

An example of tactical empathy involves a famous instance in Chris Voss's career. In 1998, while Voss was the head of the NYC FBI Crisis Negotiation team, he describes a hostage situation where for six hours, Voss spoke through the apartment door of a Harlem apartment with three heavily armed fugitives. Throughout the six-hour process, Voss calmly repeated statements such as “it looks like you don't want to come out,” and “it looks like you don't want to go back to jail.

” Voss used tactical empathy to perceive the fugitives' emotions and repeat them back to them, demonstrating to them his understanding of the situation and their feelings. Eventually, the fugitives came out of the apartment, stating that “we didn't want to get caught or get shot, but you calmed us down. We finally believed you wouldn't go away, so we just came out.

” When reflecting on the experience, Voss explains how he de-escalated the event by identifying the fugitives' emotions, labeling them, and then discussing calming with the fugitives to ensure to them that he understands their feelings and viewpoints.

“Conflict brings out truth, creativity, and resolution.”

― Chris Voss, Never Split the Difference: Negotiating As If Your Life Depended On It

Before Voss's tactical empathy approach in negotiations, traditional negotiating techniques were more stoic and strict. Voss's impact has been widespread. He has taught this style of negotiation to peers within the Federal Bureau of Investigation as well as students and business people. Chris Voss describes tactical empathy as emotional intelligence on steroids.

The ability to recognize the perspective of a counterpart and the vocalization of that recognition is key to tactical empathy in negotiation.

Labeling - Identifying emotions and stating them out loud

Labeling is an innovative negotiation technique rooted in behavioral science, which is used to diffuse situations by making others feel listened to and understood. Voss used labeling throughout his career as a negotiator and has stated that it is his most essential negotiation tactics.

“Mirrors work magic. Repeat the last three words (or the critical one to three words) of what someone has just said. We fear what's different and are drawn to what's similar. Mirroring is the art of insinuating similarity, which facilitates bonding. Use mirrors to encourage the other side to empathize and bond with you, keep people talking, buy your side time to regroup, and encourage your counterparts to reveal their strategy.”

― Chris Voss, Never Split the Difference: Negotiating as if Your Life Depended on It

Labeling in negotiations was derived from the behavioral science concept of affect labeling, an implicit emotional regulation strategy. The origins of affect labeling are unknown and date back to the beginning of psychotherapy. Keeping journals is a century-long example of affect labeling.

Voss states that the three steps to labeling which include: detecting emotional states of individuals, labeling the emotional state and saying it out loud, and finally staying silent and letting the other individual process the label.

To test the power of labeling, researchers examined the

https://thedecisionlab.com/reference-guide/psychology/amygdala

, and it's reactions to negative images. The amygdala is the nerve center for emotions in the human brain. The experiment was conducted by putting participants in fMRIs (functional magnetic resonance imaging equipment), and observing the electrical activity in their brains as they saw different photos. After showing participants a negative image, researchers then would ask them what they were feeling, and to identify that feeling and label it. The electrical activity in the negative part of the amygdala would decrease each time a participant would self-label their emotions. Researchers noted that recognizing the negative emotion and labeling lowered the impact of the negativity on the amygdala.

“He who has learned to disagree without being disagreeable has discovered the most valuable secret of negotiation.”

― Chris Voss, Never Split the Difference: Negotiating As If Your Life Depended On It

Labeling is a simple and effective method used in negotiating. Outside of negotiations, labeling has impacted disciplines ranging from social media and therapy. Voss's use of labeling in negotiation has notably changed the FBI's practices and the work of his clients and students. Labeling negative emotions aids in diffusing them, while labeling positive emotions reinforces them in negotiation. Voss states that the golden rule when negotiating with individuals is that humans all want to be appreciated and understood.

Labeling is a starting point to pursue this universal appreciation.

Historical Biography

Chris Voss, now the famous American negotiator, was born in Mt.Pleasant, Iowa.

Voss earned a Bachelor of Science from Iowa State University and then a Master's Degree in Public Administration from the John F. Kennedy School of Government.

Voss acquired his knowledge of negotiation through an array of experiences throughout his career. He held a 24-year tenure in the FBI and was trained to negotiate by the FBI, Scotland Yard, and Harvard Law School.

“If you approach a negotiation thinking the other guy thinks like you, you are wrong. That's not empathy, that's a projection.”

― Chris Voss, Never Split the Difference: Negotiating As If Your Life Depended On It

Throughout Voss's time at the Federal Bureau of Investigation, he worked on over 150 international hostage cases. Eventually, Voss led the international kidnapping negotiator team for the FBI, as their chief international hostage and kidnapping negotiator from 2003 to 2007.

Notably, Voss worked as a case agent on cases such as the TWA Flight 800 catastrophe, the TERRSTOP (the Blind Sheikh Case), and he negotiated the surrender of the first hostage in the Chase Manhattan bank robbery.

Voss retired from the FBI in 2007 and founded the Black Swan Group, a company of negotiation experts that offer negotiation training for businesses and individuals.

Voss now has a long academic career, having taught business negotiations as an adjunct professor at the Georgetown University, while also teaching at Harvard University and the University of Southern California.

Aside from the milestones and successes throughout his career, Chris Voss is most well-known for his negotiating tactics developed throughout his experiences at the FBI. His negotiation philosophy on tactical empathy is his most famous contribution. The steps of tactical empathy, involve detecting the emotional states of those you are negotiating with, labeling their emotions, and then being silent.

Voss's tactical empathy is now widely taught in schools and through his consulting services and has strongly impacted the Negotiation Unit at the FBI.

Chris Voss has worked on a handful of famous negotiation cases, including the Jill Carrol case in Iraq, and the Steve Centanni case in the Gaza Strip in 2006.

Additionally, Chris Voss has worked alongside many other prominent government officials and FBI colleagues throughout his career.

“The beauty of empathy is that it doesn't demand that you agree with the other person's ideas”

― Chris Voss, Never Split the Difference: Negotiating As If Your Life Depended On It

Conflict resolution is one of the most important skills you will ever develop, and almost nobody teaches it to you. This guide walks you through the exact tools we use at Bay Area CBT Center to help

https://bayareacbtcenter.com/couples-therapy/

https://bayareacbtcenter.com/mental-health-services/family-therapy/

resolve disagreements, repair ruptures, and build relationships that can hold difficult conversations without falling apart. Every framework here is something you can start practicing today.

Key Takeaways

Resolving conflict is a learnable life skill that matters more for the quality of your personal relationships than most things taught in school. Most adults begin learning conflict resolution skills in their 30s, 40s, or 50s, after years of painful patterns that didn't have to go that way.

Effective conflict management depends on

emotional intelligence

https://bayareacbtcenter.com/emotional-intelligence-eq/

, emotional awareness, perspective taking, and the ability to separate needs from wants during the conflict resolution process.

Nonviolent Communication (NVC)

https://bayareacbtcenter.com/nonviolent-communication/

Acceptance and Commitment Therapy (ACT)

https://bayareacbtcenter.com/acceptance-and-commitment-therapy/

are the main frameworks used at Bay Area CBT Center for resolving conflicts in couples and families in San Francisco,

Los Angeles

https://bayareacbtcenter.com/los-angeles-therapy/couples-therapy/

https://bayareacbtcenter.com/san-diego-therapy/

This article covers concrete tools: naming feelings and needs, warm vs. cold empathy, recognizing self serving fairness interpretations and cognitive distortions, using the NVC formula, the difference between requests and demands, and a step-by-step negotiation formula used in our couples therapy.

You can practice these skills at home using our

mental health exercises

https://bayareacbtcenter.com/mental-health-exercises/

, and Bay Area CBT Center also runs a

Nonviolent Communication group

https://bayareacbtcenter.com/support-groups/using-non-violent-communication-nvc-to-improve-relationships/

https://bayareacbtcenter.com/retreats/mental-health-retreat/

focused on conflict resolution skills.

Why Conflict Resolution Matters More Than Calculus

You spent years in school learning algebra, calculus, and the periodic table. You probably never had a single class on how to name what you feel, identify what you need, or negotiate a disagreement without someone ending up hurt. These are the skills that determine the quality of your relationship with your partner, your kids, your parents, and your closest friends. Conflict resolution skills boost well-being and productivity in every area of life, yet most of us have to reverse-engineer them as adults.

Consider a couple arguing every Sunday about chores. One partner feels resentful because the house is always messy. The other feels criticized no matter what they do. Without conflict resolution skills, this fight repeats for years, eroding trust and intimacy. Effective conflict resolution reduces emotional and financial costs; it also strengthens relationships and builds trust when both people feel heard. The difference between resolving conflicts (aiming for repair and mutual understanding) and just stopping an argument (shutting down, avoiding, or winning at the other person's expense) is enormous.

Many people in their 30s, 40s, and 50s arrive at therapy saying, "We never learned how to fight fair." That is a normal part of adult life, and the good news is that these are trainable skills. Conflict resolution is not a personality trait. It is a set of practices you can learn at any age.

What Is Conflict? From Arguments to Unmet Needs

Conflict is a perceived incompatibility of needs, goals, or values between two or more parties who matter to each other. It shows up when your partner is chronically late and you feel disrespected, when your teenager wants more independence and you need to know they are safe, or when siblings disagree about how to care for aging parents. Interpersonal conflict is not a sign that something is broken. It is a normal part of every close relationship. The issue is never whether conflict happens; it is how the parties involved handle conflict when it does.

Mismanaged conflict can cause irreparable rifts in relationships. When a dispute goes unaddressed, minor frustrations become major disputes. Addressing conflict early can prevent that spiral. Imagine a partner who feels hurt each time the other scrolls their phone during dinner. If that hurt is never named, it grows into resentment, then contempt, then emotional withdrawal. But when conflict is managed well, it actually deepens intimacy because both people learn more about each other's underlying interests and needs.

Strong emotions in conflict, whether rage, hurt, or shutdown, usually signal deeper unmet needs: respect, closeness, autonomy, security, or rest. Conflict resolution includes negotiation, mediation, and arbitration methods depending on the context, but in personal relationships, the goal is almost always the same: find a mutually acceptable solution where neither person feels dismissed. The couples who stay together are not the couples who avoid conflict. They are the ones who keep returning to the resolution process with willingness and care.

How Do You Usually Respond to Conflict?

Before learning new tools, it helps to recognize your default. Think about your last three arguments. When things got heated, did you attack ("You always…"), defend ("That's not what I meant!"), fix ("Here's what you should do"), shut down and

https://bayareacbtcenter.com/tips-for-recognizing-and-defending-against-passive-aggressive-behavior/

, or immediately placate and agree to anything just to end the tension?

The Thomas-Kilmann model identifies five conflict management styles: avoiding, accommodating, competing, compromising, and collaborating. Conflict management styles include avoiding, accommodating, and competing, but collaboration is a preferred conflict management style for win-win solutions. Compromising is effective when goals are moderately important, but for the issues that matter most, you want to build toward collaboration. Most people default to one or two styles without realizing it.

These patterns are often shaped by your family of origin. If you grew up in a home where anger was explosive, you may have learned to avoid conflict at all costs. If nobody in your family ever talked about hard topics, you may not have the language for what you feel. At Bay Area CBT Center, we help clients map their typical emotional reactions and behavioral patterns across recent conflicts so they can see the habit clearly before we introduce new conflict resolution skills. Awareness is always the first step.

Emotional Intelligence and Emotional Awareness in Conflict

Emotional intelligence in the context of conflict resolution means three things: recognizing what you feel, understanding what you are needing underneath that feeling, and being able to imagine what the other person might be feeling and needing. A meta-analysis of 37 studies found that emotional intelligence correlates with better conflict outcomes overall (r = .209), with the effect being even stronger for relational outcomes (r = .262) than for economic ones. In personal relationships, that relational quality is exactly what matters.

Emotional awareness is crucial for effective conflict communication. It starts with noticing sensations in the body: a tight jaw, a racing heart, a knot in the stomach. Then it moves to labeling emotions accurately. There is a big difference between hurt, anger, and shame, and each one points to a different need. Emotional awareness helps assess your emotional experience before you speak. Understanding emotions improves communication during conflicts, and emotional awareness can prevent unhealthy conflict responses like lashing out or going silent. If you struggle to name what you feel, tools like the

feeling wheel

https://bayareacbtcenter.com/the-feeling-wheel-a-practical-guide-to-understanding-your-emotions/

can help you build vocabulary.

Here is the difference in practice. Reactive version: "You never listen, you're so selfish." Emotionally aware version: "When you checked your phone during dinner, I felt invisible and hurt because I need attention and connection after work." The content is the same. The landing is completely different. Emotional awareness is crucial for effective conflict management, and it sets the foundation for everything that follows.

Warm vs. Cold Empathy: Balancing Feeling and Understanding

Empathy and validation in conflict acknowledge the other person's feelings as valid. But empathy is not one thing. Warm empathy is feeling with someone: emotional resonance where you can almost feel their sadness, fear, or joy in your own body. When your partner tears up describing a painful memory and your eyes fill too, that is warm empathy.

Mid-argument, many partners feel

ambivalence

https://bayareacbtcenter.com/understanding-ambivalence-meaning-examples-and-insights/

—wanting to repair while still protecting a wounded pride.

Cold empathy is cognitive perspective taking. You accurately understand what the other person feels and why it makes sense given their history, without necessarily feeling it yourself. For example: "It makes sense you're scared of abandonment given what happened in your last relationship." You are taking the other's perspective and seeing their other's point of view clearly.

Resolving conflicts requires both. Too much warm empathy and you get emotionally flooded, lose yourself in the other person's pain, and agree to things you later resent. Too much cold empathy and you come across as detached, like a clinician analyzing a case rather than a partner who cares. One partner in a couple might default to warm empathy, saying yes to everything in the moment, then feeling resentful for weeks. Another partner might stay entirely in cold empathy, offering perfectly logical reflections while the other feels completely alone. The skill is noticing which side you default to and intentionally dialing up the other when the moment calls for it.

https://bayareacbtcenter.com/empathy/

in conflict means you can feel with someone and still keep a clear self—neither flooded nor coldly detached.

ACT and CBT Tools for Staying Grounded During Conflict

At Bay Area CBT Center, we integrate Acceptance and Commitment Therapy and

Cognitive Behavioral Therapy

https://bayareacbtcenter.com/what-is-cognitive-behavioral-therapy-cbt/

into conflict management work with couples and families. These frameworks give people practical ways to stay grounded when emotions escalate. Managing emotions before attempting to resolve conflict helps prevent escalation, and emotional regulation helps prevent volatility during conflicts.

ACT skills for the resolution process include

mindfulness of the present moment

https://bayareacbtcenter.com/mindfulness-practices/

(noticing body sensations and urges before reacting), defusion from thoughts like "She doesn't care about me" (seeing them as mental events rather than facts), and using

core values

https://bayareacbtcenter.com/values-domains-and-values-based-actions-in-act-therapy/

like kindness and honesty to guide your behavior instead of letting the emotion decide.

CBT contributes by helping you identify automatic thoughts and cognitive distortions: mind reading ("He's doing this to hurt me"), catastrophizing ("This fight means we'll break up"), emotional reasoning ("I feel rejected, so you must be rejecting me"), and self serving fairness interpretations ("It's only fair if I get my way"). These distortions warp how we read our own emotional responses and the other party's intentions.

Here is a concrete example. During a Sunday morning argument about money, one partner notices the thought "You never respect my hard work." Instead of attacking, they pause, recognize this might be mind reading, use defusion, and choose to say: "I'm feeling unappreciated and scared about our finances. Can we talk about this?" That small shift changes the entire trajectory of the conversation.

Reading and Using Body Language in Conflict

Emotional awareness extends beyond words. Nonverbal cues can reveal true feelings during conflicts: crossed arms, averted gaze, clenched jaw, a flat tone, or a partner who says "I'm fine" while staring at the floor. Body language often communicates more than the words themselves.

In a household, this shows up constantly. A teen rolling their eyes and turning away during a conversation about curfew. A partner's shoulders rising toward their ears at the dinner table when finances come up. These signals are data. The skill is naming what you see without judgment: "I notice your shoulders are tight and you're looking away. I'm guessing you might feel hurt or annoyed?" This is far more effective than interpreting it as "You're being childish" or "Stop giving me attitude."

Matching words and body language builds trust. When someone says "I'm listening" but keeps checking their phone, the conflict resolution process breaks down. Active listening strengthens mutual understanding during conflicts, and it requires your full physical and verbal presence.

Nonviolent Communication (NVC): The Core Conflict Resolution Process

Nonviolent Communication is the primary framework we use in couples therapy and

https://bayareacbtcenter.com/support-groups/

at Bay Area CBT Center in San Francisco, Los Angeles, and San Diego. Developed by Marshall Rosenberg, NVC provides a repeatable conflict resolution process built on four steps:

Observation: What happened, without judgment or evaluation.

Feeling: How you feel about what happened.

Need: What you are needing underneath that feeling.

Request: A specific, doable ask for right now.

Here is the same message said two ways. Blame version: "You're so lazy! You never help with chores. I'm done." NVC version: "I noticed there are dishes in the sink from last night. I feel tired and overwhelmed. I need support with cleaning up. Would you be willing to handle the dishes tonight?" Using neutral and constructive language can reduce defensiveness during discussions. Using 'I' statements prevents blaming in conflicts. The content is identical; the outcome is not.

NVC also works for listening. Instead of debating the facts, you silently guess the other person's feelings and needs: "I'm guessing you're feeling overwhelmed and need more support with the kids?" This is active listening in practice, and active listening is essential for effective conflict resolution. Separating the person from the problem enables more constructive conflict resolution because you are addressing what is happening, not who is wrong.

We run an NVC workshop and mental health retreats where clients spend entire weekends practicing observation, feeling, need, and request in real-time relationship conflicts.

Needs vs. Wants: The Rule of Staying Rigid with Needs and Flexible with Wants

A need is broad and universal: affection, rest, autonomy, play, safety, understanding. A want is a specific strategy to meet that need: a hug, a nap, a walk alone, a movie night. People get stuck in conflict because they fight for a specific want instead of staying connected to the underlying need. Focusing on interests rather than positions helps identify underlying needs during conflict.

The rule is simple: be flexible with what you want and rigid with what you need. The more flexible you are about what you want, the more likely you are to get what you need.

#### Here is what this looks like in practice:

"I feel lonely. I need affection. Would you be willing to give me a hug?" "No, I'm not in a hugging mood." "I need affection. Would you be willing to give me a kiss?" "No." "Would you be willing to rub my back?" "No." "Would you be willing to play thumb wars?" "Would you be willing to rub my hair?" "Would you be willing to dance with me?" "Would you be willing to wrestle?"

The need for affection stays constant. The wants keep changing until one lands.

#### Second example with rest:

"I feel exhausted. I need rest. Would you be willing to take the kids Saturday morning?" "I can't, I have a meeting." "I need rest. Would you be willing to handle dinner tonight so I can lie down?" "No, I'm wiped out too." "I need rest. Would you be willing to take a walk with me so I can decompress?" "Yeah, I can do that."

Healthy conflict resolution leads to creative problem solving. Recognizing conflicting needs fosters creative problem solving because both partners keep offering new ideas rather than escalating over who is right. This is how finding solutions becomes collaborative rather than adversarial.

Requests vs. Demands: How to Ask Without Punishing "No"

A true request gives the other person the freedom to say no. A demand is framed as a request, but no is punished with guilt, blame, withdrawal, or threats. You usually do not know whether your ask was a request or a demand until you hear no and see how you respond.

#### Here is the difference:

Request version: "Would you be willing to handle bedtime tonight? I need rest." "No, I'm really tired too." "Okay. I still need rest. Would you be willing to do the dishes so I can lie down while the kids play?"

Demand version: "Would you be willing to handle bedtime tonight?" "No, I'm really tired too." "Seriously? I would never say no to you. You always put yourself first. Fine, I'll just do everything myself."

If the response to no is guilt tripping, assigning blame, attacking, or scorekeeping, then it was never a request. In couples therapy at Bay Area CBT Center, we often rehearse hearing "no" in session so both parties can practice tolerating disappointment, staying in emotional awareness, and continuing the resolution process creatively instead of escalating.

The Bay Area CBT Center Negotiation Formula for Couples

When simple NVC requests are not enough and a couple is stuck in a loop around the same issue month after month, we use a four-step negotiation formula. This process works for recurring disputes about vacations, money, chores, parenting, intimacy, or how weekends are spent.

The formula assumes that in a healthy relationship, nobody gets their pure ideal. Instead, both involved parties move toward close-enough solutions while ensuring neither ends up in an intolerable situation. The interest-based relational approach focuses on mutual concerns, not on winning. This negotiation formula is part of the conflict resolution process we teach in therapy,

executive coaching

https://bayareacbtcenter.com/executive-coaching/

for leaders in California, and in our mental health retreats.

Step 1: Choose Your Values Before You Negotiate

Before discussing specifics, each partner separately chooses five values they want to embody during the negotiation. For example: kind, present, respectful, assertive, and honest. These values act as a compass throughout the process.

One partner in a San Francisco couple might choose curiosity, calm, honesty, collaboration, and playfulness. The other chooses self-respect, courage, patience, empathy, and clarity. When emotions rise, either person can pause and ask: "Is what I'm about to say aligned with 'respectful and honest,' or is it more aligned with 'winning'?"

We often have couples write these values on a notepad on the coffee table or in a shared document during telehealth sessions so they remain visible throughout the conflict resolution process. The values don't prevent disagreements. They shape how the conflicting parties move through them.

Step 2: Write Your Ideal and Your Intolerable

Each person privately writes down two things: their ideal (very specific, "if I could have it exactly my way") and their intolerable (general conditions they cannot live with long term). These must not be identical.

#### Hawaii Christmas example:

Ideal: "Every Christmas we go to Hawaii for seven days, stay at the Hilton, swim with dolphins, and eat chocolate cake every day."

Intolerable: "Not having at least one week-long vacation per year. Not going somewhere warm at least once a year. Not having some kind of fancy dessert on vacations."

Notice the ideal is hyper-specific (Hawaii, Hilton, dolphins, chocolate cake daily). The intolerable is general (a week-long vacation, warmth, a nice dessert). The gap between them is where negotiation lives.

#### Chores and weekend example:

Partner A's ideal: "Every Saturday I sleep in until 10 a.m., you take the kids to the park, and I have a quiet house."

Partner A's intolerable: "Never getting a morning to rest. Always feeling like I'm on duty."

Partner B's ideal: "We do family activities together every Saturday morning."

Partner B's intolerable: "Feeling like I have to handle all kid activities alone every weekend."

People with a perfectionism or

unrelenting standards schema

https://bayareacbtcenter.com/unpacking-the-unrelenting-standards-schema/

entitlement schema

https://bayareacbtcenter.com/understanding-the-entitlement-grandiosity-schema/

, often struggle here and write the same thing for both columns. When the intolerable is the same as the ideal, there is nothing to negotiate. This is a point where therapy can help loosen rigid thinking.

Step 3: Share, Commit, and Reframe the Goal

Both partners share their written ideal and intolerable lists, ideally in a calm setting: at the kitchen table on a Sunday afternoon, not at midnight after a fight.

Then each person says to the other: "I am willing to let go of my ideal and negotiate with you to get close enough to my ideal, and I am committed to making sure you are not in an intolerable situation."

This single statement reframes the entire negotiation. The goal is not "Whose ideal is more reasonable?" or "I win and you lose." The goal is: both parties agree to avoid intolerable outcomes and get close enough to their ideals to feel cared for. One partner realizes that dolphins and a specific hotel are wants, not needs, while warmth, vacation length, and dessert are needs. The other partner shares an ideal like "Spending Christmas with extended family locally," with an intolerable of "Not seeing my parents at all during winter holidays." Now both people can see the overlap and the differences clearly.

Step 4: Brainstorm the Middle and Land on a Creative Solution

Everything between the two ideals is brainstorm space. Both people list every possible option without criticizing or rejecting any idea, as long as no proposal falls into the other person's intolerable zone.

#### Brainstorming list for the vacation example:

Palm Springs in January

Mexico in February

Local spa week with day trips

Visit family on Christmas, take a warm trip in March

Shorter warm trip plus a weekend with extended family

Rent an Airbnb somewhere warm over New Year's

Once every idea is written down, you start crossing options out together and land on a creative solution. Maybe the outcome is: a week in Palm Springs in January with a nice dessert each night, Christmas with family in California, and dolphins dropped from the plan because they were a want, not a need. That is a negotiated agreement where neither person is in an intolerable situation and both are close enough to their ideal.

For the chores and weekend example, the resolution might be alternating Saturdays where one partner sleeps in and the other handles kids, plus one shared family activity on Sunday morning. Neither is constantly exhausted, and neither is constantly alone with responsibilities. Documenting agreements and following up can prevent misunderstandings, so write down what you decided and revisit it in a month.

This negotiation formula can be revisited yearly, for instance before holidays, and is a repeatable conflict resolution process for any recurring issue. New ideas emerge when both people brainstorm freely, and the outcome tends to feel more fair than any quick solution imposed by one or both parties.

Self-Compassion and Mindfulness: Staying Present Instead of Attacking or Shutting Down

Even with good tools, conflict is emotionally painful. It can trigger shame, fear, or old trauma. Without self-compassion, people tend to either shut down ("I'm a terrible partner, I give up") or attack ("This is all your fault"). Stress limits emotional awareness and conflict resolution ability, which is why quick stress relief helps maintain calm during conflicts.

Self-compassion in this context means talking to yourself the way you would talk to a close friend: acknowledging that conflict is hard, that you are learning, and that mistakes are part of growth. Here is a simple practice: during a heated argument, pause. Feel your feet on the ground. Place a hand on your chest. Silently say, "This is a hard moment. Lots of couples struggle with this. I'm doing my best to stay aligned with my values."

Mindfulness

https://bayareacbtcenter.com/meditation/

, meaning present-moment awareness of breath, body, and emotions, supports emotional regulation by helping you notice urges to criticize, withdraw, or defend before acting on them. At Bay Area CBT Center, we integrate mindfulness,

https://bayareacbtcenter.com/san-francisco-therapy/dbt-therapy/

like distress tolerance, and

compassion-focused practices

https://bayareacbtcenter.com/compassion-focused-therapy/

https://bayareacbtcenter.com/individual-sessions/

couples therapy

https://bayareacbtcenter.com/how-couples-therapy-at-bay-area-cbt-center-fortifies-your-relationship-bond/

, as well as in groups and

https://bayareacbtcenter.com/retreats/

, to strengthen these conflict resolution skills. You can also explore

self-improvement activities

https://bayareacbtcenter.com/self-improvement-activities/

to build these habits on your own.

How Bay Area CBT Center Can Help You Build Conflict Resolution Skills

Conflict management is not just about tools. It is also about healing patterns rooted in anxiety, depression, trauma,

attachment injuries

https://bayareacbtcenter.com/insecure-attachment-and-relational-trauma/

https://bayareacbtcenter.com/schema-therapy/

like perfectionism, abandonment, or mistrust. Many people view conflict through the lens of old wounds, and the resolution process requires both skill-building and deeper therapeutic work.

At Bay Area CBT Center, we offer evidence-based couples therapy, family therapy,

discernment counseling

https://bayareacbtcenter.com/discernment-counseling/

, group therapy focused on Nonviolent Communication, executive coaching for leaders managing high-stakes relational conflicts, and mental health retreats where participants spend entire weekends immersed in NVC and ACT skills. We work with individuals and couples across California, including San Francisco,

https://bayareacbtcenter.com/oakland-therapy-counseling/couples-marriage-therapy/

, Los Angeles, and San Diego, via in-person and

online therapy

https://bayareacbtcenter.com/locations/california-online-therapy/

NVC worksheets page

https://bayareacbtcenter.com/mental-health-exercises/nvc-worksheets/

for practices you can do at home: worksheets to build emotional awareness, identify values, track thoughts, and rehearse the conflict resolution process on your own. If you recognize entrenched patterns like constant escalation, conflict avoidance, or emotional volatility, consider

reaching out

https://bayareacbtcenter.com/book-free-appointment/

for therapy or coaching. A neutral third party may facilitate a difficult conversation when necessary, and using a neutral third party can help both parties move past the stuck points that self-help alone cannot reach. Successful conflict resolution is possible. It starts with a willingness to learn and a decision to practice.

Along with therapy, Bay Area CBT Center offers

https://bayareacbtcenter.com/tests/

you can take on your own.

When is the best time to try to resolve a conflict with my partner?

The conflict resolution process works best when both nervous systems are somewhat regulated, not at peak anger or shutdown. If a conversation has escalated, suggest waiting 20 to 60 minutes before re-engaging. Agree ahead of time, not during a fight, on a pause phrase like "Let's take a 30-minute break and come back using NVC," and honor that agreement consistently. Leaving for days without communication is not skillful cooling off; it is conflict avoidance. Set a clear time to revisit the conversation later the same day or the next morning. The goal is to resolve disagreements, not to let them go underground where they grow.

How do I practice conflict resolution skills if my partner doesn't want to read about this or come to therapy?

You can still shift the pattern by changing your side. Start naming your feelings and needs, shift from blame to NVC, and respond to "no" without punishing it. Replace "You never help" with "I feel overwhelmed and need more support with bedtime. Would you be willing to handle baths tonight?" Even if the other party doesn't use the framework, they will likely respond differently to a request than to an accusation. Focus on consistency over perfection. Over weeks or months, many partners naturally begin to mirror these new ways of resolving disputes when they experience less blame and more clarity. One person changing how they communicate effectively is often enough to shift the entire dynamic.

What should we do if the conflict keeps coming back even after using these tools?

Recurring arguments about the same topic, whether money, intimacy, in-laws, parenting, or chores, may point to deeper values clashes, attachment wounds, or unprocessed trauma. These are the root cause issues that surface-level tools alone may not fully address. Try the Bay Area CBT Center negotiation formula (values, ideal vs. intolerable, brainstorming) if you haven't yet, especially for long-standing disputes about holidays or the division of labor. If the issue still feels stuck after several sincere attempts at mutually acceptable agreements, consider seeking couples therapy or family therapy, online or in person. A therapist can help identify hidden beliefs or schemas that keep the dispute locked in place and guide both differing perspectives toward a resolution that sticks.

How can we teach conflict resolution skills to our kids at home?

Model NVC and emotional awareness out loud. Let your children hear you say, "I feel frustrated because I need cooperation. My request is that we all put our shoes by the door." When kids hear feelings, needs, and requests named explicitly, they internalize the framework naturally. Help a child practice: "I feel mad. I need a turn. Can we set a timer?" instead of grabbing a toy. Show siblings how to brainstorm multiple wants that could meet both kids' needs. Practicing these skills in family meetings or weekly check-ins normalizes resolving disputes respectfully and gives kids

emotional intelligence skills

https://bayareacbtcenter.com/building-emotional-intelligence-with-a-dbt-therapist/

they are unlikely to get from school. Even brief, consistent practice in beneficial relationships at home builds habits that last a lifetime.

How do I know if our conflicts have become emotionally abusive or unsafe?

Warning signs include repeated insults, name-calling, threats, controlling behavior such as monitoring a partner's phone or finances, intimidation, or any physical aggression or even violence that makes one or both parties feel unsafe. These patterns go beyond difficult conversations and into territory where safety is the priority, not better communication skills. In situations of emotional or physical abuse, the strategies in this article assume a basic commitment to mutual respect between two parties. If that foundation is absent, the focus needs to shift to safety planning and individual support. If you notice these patterns, reach out to a therapist, a trusted friend, or a domestic violence hotline in California. Individual counseling at Bay Area CBT Center can support you in safety planning, healing, and building toward a better understanding of what a healthy relationship can look like.

Frequently Asked Questions

What is evidence-based therapy?+–

Evidence-based therapy involves interventions that are scientifically proven to be effective for particular issues. In this approach, a strong partnership based on trust and collaboration is formed between you and your therapist. Within this supportive and unbiased environment, you can freely express yourself without fear of judgment. Over a series of sessions, you and your therapist will work together to address obstacles and set goals aimed at personal growth and fulfillment. This method ensures that the techniques and strategies used are not only supportive but also empirically validated to help you achieve your therapeutic goals.

Who do we work with?+–

The Bay Area CBT Center provides therapy services for everyone, from children to adults, and welcomes individuals, couples, and groups. We help with various concerns like anxiety, depression, trauma, relationship issues, and behavior challenges. We value diversity and cultural differences, offering personalized and culturally sensitive care to each client.

Why is it so important to find the right therapist?+–

Studies show that the bond between you and your therapist, known as the therapeutic alliance, is a key factor in treatment success. This alliance is characterized by the strength of your relationship and how well you both agree on treatment goals. Research indicates that individuals with a solid therapeutic alliance experience better treatment outcomes including greater productivity at work, more satisfying relationships, improved stress management, and decreased engagement in risky behaviors.

What should I expect from the matching appointment?+–

You can expect a 15-30 minute phone call with our care coordinator, who is extensively trained in ensuring the perfect match for you. During this conversation, our matching expert will collaborate with you to understand your therapy needs, preferences, and scheduling availability. This discussion builds upon the information you provided during sign-up and offers an opportunity for you to address any personal questions or concerns you may have about therapy or our services at The Bay Area CBT Center. Following your conversation, we'll pair you with the therapist who best aligns with your needs, goals, and preferences.

When will I be matched to a Therapist?+–

At your matching appointment, we will match you with a therapist specifically chosen for you and schedule your first session. Depending on your availability, you can expect to meet your therapist anywhere from one day to a week after this appointment.

Do you provide in-person or virtual therapy?+–

Our approach to therapy includes a flexible hybrid model, blending both online and face-to-face sessions. This option is perfect for clients situated close to our clinics in the Bay Area who prefer the flexibility of choosing between virtual consultations or meeting their therapist in person. Our aim with hybrid care is to ensure every client is matched with the ideal therapist and therapy environment, be it from the convenience of your own home or in one of our clinics.

Do you accept insurance?+–

At the Bay Area CBT Center, we accept PPO insurance plans that allow you to use out-of-network providers. This means if your insurance plan is a PPO and it includes mental health benefits, you could get back some or all of the money you pay for our services, depending on what your insurance company allows. When you see one of our therapists, they'll give you a superbill. You can send this superbill to your insurance company to ask for reimbursement. If you're not sure if your insurance covers services from providers not in their network, it's a good idea to give them a call and check. You may be eligible to have 60-80% of your costs covered by out-of-network benefits. Also, if you have an FSA (Flexible Spending Account), you can usually use it to pay for individual counseling sessions. It's wise to double-check with your FSA provider or talk to your accountant to make sure that counseling sessions are considered an allowed expense.

https://www.linkedin.com/in/cortex-copywriter/

Enhance your UX Writing with FBI Negotiation Tactics

What do FBI hostage negotiators and UX writers have in common? They both guide human behavior by using techniques in psychology and empathy – understanding another person's situation, motives, feelings, and point of view.

UX writers are involved in negotiations all the time. How do we get users to complete a form or subscribe to a newsletter? While this is not the high stakes world of international kidnapping, FBI negotiating tactics can help you in business, family life, and even UX writing.

Chris Voss, founder and CEO of The Black Swan Group and author of

Never Split the Difference

, is a former FBI lead international kidnapping negotiator. During his 20 years at the FBI, Voss resolved kidnappings involving mercenaries, drug dealers, and terrorists using a system he helped design – tactical empathy.

Create Trust with Tactical Empathy

The question of why the FBI's techniques worked drew Voss to Harvard University. Voss discovered that his tactical empathy system can be explained by research in psychology, neuroscience, and behavioral economics.

Tactical empathy takes emotional intelligence and intuition to the next level. It's understanding the feelings and mindset of another person so you can identify what drives those feelings and influence them.

Let's explore tactical empathy and look at examples of how to apply it in UX writing.

Establish Rapport with a Mirror

The FBI hostage negotiation skill of mirroring is almost like a Jedi mind trick. Mirroring is when you repeat exactly the last 3 words of what someone has just said. But when you get good at mirroring, you can pick one to three words from anywhere in the conversation.

We fear what is different and find comfort in similarity. When you mirror someone, they feel comforted, they're encouraged to empathize and bond with you.

A study by psychologist Richard Wiseman showed that waiters who used mirroring received a 70% larger average tip than waiters who used positive reinforcement (saying "sure", "no problem", or "great" in response to each order).

Check out the

'Switch experience'

button on the

https://www.cortexcopywriter.com/index.html

of this website to see examples of mirroring. The switch button lets you change the web copy, chatbot dialogue, techniques examples, and more to mirror the exact words, voice, and tone used by different groups of people (Technology, Corporate, Luxury, Community, Entertainment).

Mirroring people's words in web copy and chatbot dialogue

Here's how Nike used mirroring to create one of my favorite pieces of copy. Have you ever used the word “tomorrow” to put things off?

Tomorrow I'll go to the gym.

Tomorrow I'll start writing that book.

Tomorrow I'll quit my job and start my own business.

Nike took the word “tomorrow” and reflected it back to us in an advertisement that motivates people to stop procrastinating – go out and get fit today.

Oh, the guilt!

Put a Label on it

The first step to labeling is simply being aware of another person's emotional state. Once you spot an emotion, turn it into words. Labels can be phrased as statements or questions that usually begin with the following phrases:

Phrases like

“It sounds like you're afraid of…”

“It looks like you're concerned about…”

help diffuse negative emotions.

Labels can reinforce positive perceptions and dynamics. The golden rule is to understand that you're dealing with a human who wants to be appreciated.

Here's a Goibibo error message that uses labeling and a conversational tone to help guide users.

Goibibo's label is clear, concise, and conversational

Summarize the Situation

Summary is the combination of labels and paraphrasing. Paraphrase is taking what someone says and putting it in your own words (whereas mirroring doesn't change the person's words). This proves to the other person that you really do understand them and aren't merely parroting their concerns.

You simply summarize both the known facts and content (paraphrasing) about a situation, and how the other person feels about them (labeling).

An example of summary is Spotify's Wrapped feature. At the end of each year, Spotify calculates how much time you spent listening to music and which songs and artists you streamed the most.

The Wrapped feature summarizes users' top songs, hours listened, top artists, and much more in all sorts of fun infographics.

People love to be listened to. It's no surprise people share their Wrapped findings on social media even if they're not particularly proud of which songs they listened to most throughout the year.

Spotify summarizes your great taste in music with paraphrase and labels

The 2 Magic Words that Transform any Negotiation

Chris Voss analyzed transcripts from his team's successful hostage negotiations and made an important discovery. The turning point in negotiations usually occurred after the team listened to the captor's words, summarized the words back to the captor, and got the captor to say, “That's right.”

This supports the writings of American psychologist Carl Rogers. His teachings on empathy guided people to summarize their counterpart's thoughts and feelings so fully and completely that there is only one possible response – “That's right”.

These two words signal that your negotiating partner feels acknowledged. A chemical change takes place in their brain causing a subtle epiphany – they've heard the complete and indisputable truth. They are willing to listen, and they may not even be aware of releasing their resistance to you.

If you can get someone to trust that you understand where they are coming from, you have a much greater chance of getting what you want.

Let's take a look at how Fibery gets users to a “That's right” epiphany. Fibery's landing page has a button ('I don't get it, explain differently') that lets you switch the web copy voice and tone. One of the voices sounds like a self-deprecating developer riddled with anxiety. This unique and hilarious voice makes users feel heard and acknowledged.

Here is one developer's comment in response to the tagline – Try. Suffer. Quit. – on the fibery.io/anxiety web page:

“That's my career in 3 words”

. This comment is very close to “That's right”.

#### Fibery's landing page went viral on Twitter:

“As if someone opened a window and let out the thick stale rank of bullshit we've all gotten used to”

. Fibery's brutal honesty and deep understanding of their target audience helps them get what they want – more people signing up to their platform.

If only all landing pages were this honest

Master “no”

According to Voss, “yes” is a horrible word because it scares people. We're so used to being trapped by “yes” that we get nervous and wonder what we committed to. Allowing people to say “no” makes them feel relaxed and protected.

“No” respects people's autonomy and gives them the illusion of control. Simply change your 'yes-oriented' questions –

“Is it ok if we sit in the reserved section?”

to 'no-oriented' questions –

“Would it be horrible if we sat in this section?”

#### Most 'no-oriented' questions begin with:

“Is it ridiculous...?”

“Do you disagree…?”

“Is it a bad idea...?”

“Are you against…?”

“Am I out of line...?”

“Have you given up on this project?”

is the highest converting email subject line used by Voss's clients. It triggers the safety of “no” and boosts it with loss aversion (people will take greater risks to avoid losses than to achieve gains).

Here's a notification from Headspace meditation & sleep app which uses a 'yes-oriented' question. It raises a lot of questions like:

"How long is a few minutes?"

"If I have a few minutes, do I want to spend it on this app?"

A flat-out “yes” can be a scary thing to say

Here I have rephrased the notification as a 'no-oriented' question to help users relax and focus better on the message. It opens a path forward, increasing the chances you will continue using the app.

“No” is the start of the negotiation, not the end of it

Bending Reality

Voss teaches the art of bending people's reality with neuroscience. He has a variety of tools for framing a negotiation in a way that your counterpart will unconsciously accept the limits you place on the discussion.

For example, when you smile at someone, they'll smile back as a reflex. Conversations seem to flow when you radiate warmth and acceptance. You can use your voice to reach into someone's brain and flip an emotional switch – distrusting to trusting, or nervous to calm.

This also works in digital interfaces when you're not actually facing someone. You can write copy with a conversational tone – the same used with family and friends – to stimulate the release of feelgood chemicals (oxytocin, dopamine).

Here's some microcopy from Slack that speaks to users respectfully, in a humble and honest way that generates feelings of safety and builds trust.

Slack makes you feel calm when something goes wrong

Numbers have psychological significance. In terms of negotiation, numbers ending in 0 feel like temporary placeholders or guesses. But less rounded numbers (e.g. $72,589) feel like a figure that you came to as a result of thoughtful calculation.

Here's an example of how Visme uses odd numbers to create a feeling of legitimacy.

Odd numbers feel serious and permanent

You can put someone in a generous mood by making them an offer, then after they reject it, give them an unrelated surprise gift. Unexpected gestures are effective because they introduce a dynamic called reciprocity; we pay back what we receive from others.

Here is ClickUp's welcome email offering users a surprise – 'warm chocolate cookies' (a link in the email that takes you to a delicious cookie recipe) – which makes users want to repay the gift by exploring ClickUp further.

People feel obliged to repay debts of kindness

Create Breakthroughs by Revealing Black Swans

In any negotiation, success is determined by how well you listen, not how well you speak. According to Voss, there's the visible negotiation and the secret negotiation space under the surface where the 'Black Swans' live.

Black Swans are pieces of information, subtle truths, that when discovered, shift a negotiation in your favor.

Access to this hidden space comes through understanding people's worldview, their 'religion' – the authority that determines what is fair to them – the market, experts, society, or God.

Once you understand your counterpart's worldview, you can build influence.

In March 2003, Voss led the negotiation with Dwight Watson, a farmer whose bomb threat brought Washington, D.C. to a standstill. Rather than engage Watson in logical arguments and force him to surrender, Voss attempted to unearth Watson's worldview.

An FBI agent on Voss's team listened intently to subtle references that Watson made, the agent told Voss:

“Watson is a devout Christian”. Tell him tomorrow is the Dawn of the Third Day. That's the day Christians believe Jesus Christ left his tomb and ascended to Heaven. If Christ came out on the Dawn of the Third Day, why not Watson?”

By combining the subtext of Watson's words with knowledge of his worldview, the FBI made Watson feel heard. In their next conversation, Watson said

“I'll come out”

. He did, ending a forty-eight-hour standoff.

Voss teaches his clients that engaging someone face-to-face is the best way to discover Black Swans. But in today's era of personalized marketing, companies can use data mining techniques to gain a holistic understanding of their customers' worldview and fill databases with Black Swans.

Here's a piece of microcopy that shows how deeply Best Buy listens to and understands their customers. Somehow, Best Buy knows whether you celebrate Hanukkah (Dec. 22-30), Christmas (Dec. 25), or Kwanzaa (Dec. 26-Jan. 1). If you don't observe Christmas, you can feel alienated when online stores say 'Arrives by Christmas'. That's why people appreciate Best Buy's 'Get it by Hanukkah' microcopy.

Best Buy knows which holiday you observe

Best Buy positions themselves within the worldview customers use to make decisions. Their Black Swan microcopy shows respect for customers' beliefs; this helps Best Buy increase conversions and sales.

It looks like you want help enhancing your writing with FBI negotiation tactics. Would it be horrible if you messaged me now? (See what I did there? A label and a 'no-oriented' question!).

Pick my brain/cortex and let's get started on your project

\[MUSIC PLAYING\] - The amygdala is this little organ in the middle of your head, and it is the nerve center of all the emotions. There were neuroscience experiments that were done where they put people in fMRIs, functional magnetic resonance imaging equipment, so they could watch the electrical activity in people's brains. Then they induced the negative emotion by showing a photo to them. The photo might make them feel sad, angry, lonely, hurt, upset. They knew the photo would trigger some sort of a negative emotion. They showed the people the photo, and they simply said, what are you feeling? They told them to identify it or label it. And every time that the person self-labeled, they saw the electrical activity in the negative part of the amygdala decrease, every time. Now, when we first started on labels in a hostage negotiation, we called them emotion labels, and we used it as a very self-defining skill. You labeled emotions. You heard an emotion, put a label on it. If somebody sounded angry, you said, you sound angry. And if that negative emotion is there, that label will reduce that negative emotion. So in this session, I'm going to teach you about labels. Labels are just verbal observations. When I first came out of hostage negotiation into business negotiation, I actually thought this was the least important skill on our list. Having been involved in business negotiation for quite a while and coaching a lot of people, we found out that labels might be the most important skill, the most innocuous skill, and the most MacGyver skill, the most useful tool with the most different applications to unwind and solve some of the trickiest problems. Don't be fooled by their simplicity. Their simplicity gives them elegance. You'll enjoy learning labels. \[MUSIC PLAYING\] The steps to labeling. The first step is you simply being aware of the emotion or the dynamic from the other side. Now, this is a lot easier than it sounds because you've got a great gut instinct. Becoming aware of what your gut instinct is picking up is the first step. Then the second step after that is to simply label it. That starts out with it seems like, it sounds like, it looks like, it feels like. Those are the first few words of a label, and then just fill in the blank. If your gut instinct is to say, look, I don't want you to be upset about this, your gut instinct is picking up that they are upset or they will be upset. So take it and make it a label. It seems like you're upset about this. What happens in your brain is you say to yourself, am I upset? It triggers that contemplation. And the mere act of triggering that contemplation deactivates the negative. So my labels are going to do several things simultaneously. They're going to build the relationship, and they're going to help me gather information. And since both of those things are happening, then my influence with you is going to increase. And the great thing about this type of influence, ...

Here's the uncomfortable truth: most negotiation training is based on a model from 1981. Harvard's "Getting to Yes" was groundbreaking at the time, but neuroscience and behavioural economics have fundamentally changed what we know about how decisions are made.

We don't teach win-win. We don't worship BATNA. Instead, we train modern techniques drawn from Chris Voss, Jim Camp, and the latest neuroscience research — including MRI studies showing how the brain processes threats, trust and decision-making during negotiations.

Win-win is misleading and dangerous. Forget BATNA.

Here's what's changed; and why it matters.

❌ Old approach (1981)

"Win-win" is always the goal

Focus on BATNA and positions

Separate people from the problem

Rational actors making logical decisions

Information sharing builds trust

Compromise is a good outcome

✓ Modern approach (neuroscience-backed)

Understand the brain's threat responses

Tactical empathy creates breakthroughs

Focus on people and what they want

Emotions drives decisions

Stop telling, start asking

No deal is better than a bad deal

VP, Compensation & Benefits

Common questions

Why do you say win-win is wrong?

We don't say it's wrong — we say it's incomplete and often misapplied. "Win-win" sounds nice but it often leads to weak compromises where neither side gets what they really need. Modern approaches focus on deeply understanding the other side's world, which often reveals creative solutions that go far beyond splitting the difference.

What's different about your negotiation training?

Three things: 1) We teach modern techniques from Chris Voss, Jim Camp and neuroscience research — not the 1981 Harvard model. 2) Everything is practice-based — participants negotiate multiple times during every session. 3) We use our free Negotiation Styles tool to help participants understand their natural tendencies before the training even starts.

Is the free negotiation styles tool really free?

Completely free. No credit card, no obligation. It's 23 questions, takes about 5 minutes, and gives you instant results about your natural negotiation tendencies. We built it because we believe self-awareness is the first step to better negotiation.

Can you use our real deals as case studies?

Absolutely — and this is where our training becomes most powerful. When participants negotiate scenarios based on their actual work, the learning transfers immediately. We work with you in advance to develop relevant, realistic scenarios.

What industries have you trained?

Banking (HSBC, Morgan Stanley, Deutsche Bank, UBS, J.P. Morgan, Barclays, Standard Chartered), insurance, asset management, professional services, technology, FMCG and many more. Negotiation principles are universal — the application is always tailored.
